A successful refresh persists to the STORE before the live file (`persist_refreshed_grok_snapshot`) — the refresh may have spent the old token, so a crash between the two writes must leave the durable record holding the live one. `apply_grok_token_response` is split from the HTTP call precisely so the credential-destroying part is unit-tested; `refresh_grok_auth` carries a `#[cfg(test)]` stub seam (`GROK_REFRESH_STUB`, same shape as grok's `WRITE_FAULT_PATH`) so switch tests exercise all three branches without touching auth.x.ai — they were hitting the real endpoint before it existed.

**Grok "Add account" / Re-login**: unconditional pre-snapshot of the live login (a successful login replaces the scope entry, taking the outgoing account's only fresh RT with it — same reason as Claude's) → spawn → wait (5 min / cancel kills the child) → save → restore the previous account via `switch_account` (skipped when the user re-logged into the SAME id). **`--device-auth` is REQUIRED, not a preference**: the loopback branch runs with `reauth=true` and CLEARS the credential up front, so abandoning that login logs the user out (`auth/flow.rs:1093`); the device branch passes `force_interactive`, which skips the clear. It is also what prints the verification URL, i.e. what makes the flow headless. **No pre-clear of auth.json** (unlike codex): grok's logout is purely LOCAL — `auth/flow.rs:992` `perform_logout` → `remove_scope`/`clear`, and the crate has no revocation endpoint at all — so saved snapshots' tokens are never at risk. The prompt goes to **stderr** (`auth/device_code.rs:365-391`), read line-by-line: a line starting with `https://` → `grok:login-url`; the next non-empty line after a heading ending in `code:` → `grok:login-code` (positional, so the code's own format is never assumed).

**Formerly a known residual, now CLOSED by the account auto-sync** (see "Automatic sync of the live account" below): changing the live grok account OUTSIDE Termory (`grok login` in a terminal) destroys the outgoing account's fresh RT at that moment and used to leave its store entry stale, so switching back failed the refresh and flagged `needsRelogin`. It was accepted because closing it appeared to need the store synced on every account-list read — a write on the tray's main-thread `build_menu` path. The auto-sync reaches it from a different direction entirely (a timer and the filesystem watcher, both off the main thread and both independent of the menu), so that objection does not apply to it. Verified end-to-end on real data 2026-08-03: a terminal `grok login` produced a `syncedAt` on the matching entry within ~1s, with `savedAt` untouched.

**Automatic sync of the live account** (`accounts.rs`, the block above `switch_account`). A saved account is a copy of the CLI's official credential, and the CLI keeps rewriting that credential underneath us — a token refresh rotates the tokens, a re-login replaces the whole thing, a plan change arrives as new claims inside the refreshed token. So the entry for the account currently in use goes stale with nothing the user did to cause it, and a stale entry is a switch-back that fails. This re-derives that ONE entry from the live credential IN FULL, through the same `*_entry` builder the Save button uses — which is the point: `name`/`email`/`plan` are parsed out of the very document that becomes the payload, so refreshing the document refreshes them together and they cannot drift apart. `codex_entry` / `claude_entry` / `grok_entry` were extracted from the three save fns for exactly this sharing.

- **The watcher is the main path for EVERY CLI, macOS Claude included.** Its credential-file branch (`watcher.rs`, beside `force_quota_refresh`, off-thread via `spawn_blocking` — the read can spawn `security(1)` and that loop still has a burst to drain) reacts in ~1s. Claude's credential is in the **Keychain** and emits nothing itself, but two files beside the config dir move when it changes — and they are routed SEPARATELY, because only one of them is a credential. **`<config-dir>.lock`** (`quota::claude_credential_signal_path`, part of `credential_cli_for_path`), which `proper-lockfile` creates by `mkdir` (lib/lockfile.js:11,:29) and which Claude takes for exactly ONE thing — refreshing the OAuth token (auth.ts:1485-1491; every other `lockfile.lock` call in its source locks a mailbox, a marker or a task, so the name is an exclusive signal, and a plan change rides that same refresh). **`.claude.json`** (`quota::claude_identity_signal_path`, matched by `watcher::event_touches_claude_identity` — kept a separate helper from `event_credential_clis` precisely so the two routes stay separable, and tested against each other's input) is the only file a LOGIN touches (`storeOAuthAccountInfo`, cli/handlers/auth.ts:58/72 — its tokens go to the Keychain). **It must NOT join `credential_cli_for_path` (LOCKED).** That map also feeds `force_quota_refresh`, which bypasses the quota's own two-minute floor, and `.claude.json` is Claude's whole global config — written from 159 places in its source, startup counters and skill-usage tracking included. Routing it there (as the first version did) turns a feature documented as having "THREE triggers (NO periodic polling)" into an Anthropic API call every ten seconds while Claude is in use. The sync can afford it where the quota cannot: a pass that finds nothing costs one credential read, writes nothing, and the watcher's debounce collapses a flurry of config writes into one. Both are matched by FULL path, since these are ordinary-looking names in the user's home. Both sit one level ABOVE the watched config tree and the lock exists only while held, so a direct file watch is impossible — `watcher::credential_signal_parents` watches their parent NON-RECURSIVELY with a name filter, the same shape (and for the same reason) as the Claude Desktop install targets. That parent is `$HOME` in the default layout, which churns constantly (`.zsh_history` on every shell command), so it also joins the rescan gate's ignore list — otherwise each of those writes would trigger a full session scan.
- **The only other trigger is ONE catch-up pass at launch** (`sync_accounts_at_launch`, from `lib.rs` setup) — for what no event can reach, namely whatever changed while Termory was closed and nobody was listening. **There is deliberately NO periodic pass**, and this is the codebase's own stance rather than a preference: the quota has "THREE triggers (NO periodic polling)", the install detection exists to have replaced a 3s poll, and session scanning leans on the watcher plus the user showing up. A timer here was written first (60s, then 300s) and removed once the Claude signal files landed — it would have been the only recurring task in the process, spawning `security(1)` forever to re-find what the watcher already found, and it held this one feature to a standard nothing else here is held to. If an event is ever missed, the exposure is the same one every other watcher-driven feature already accepts.
- **`syncedAt` is the auto-sync's OWN field, `savedAt` is not (LOCKED).** They sit side by side on the entry and have different owners: `savedAt` is written by USER actions (Save, a switch's re-snapshot, the login flow), `syncedAt` only by this flow. Each writer replaces the whole entry, so each must `carry_over` the other's field — a manual save that wiped `syncedAt` would erase the evidence the sync ever ran, and a sync that restamped `savedAt` would claim a save nobody made. This is what makes the store answerable: **absent `syncedAt` = never auto-updated**; `syncedAt` > `savedAt` = the sync has moved past your last manual save. It records a real UPDATE, not a check — a pass that found nothing leaves no trace, which is also why the field costs no extra writes. **Both are excluded from `entry_content_matches`** along with `needsRelogin`: `syncedAt` is on the stored entry but never on the freshly built one, so letting it into the comparison makes every later pass differ — a store write and a "synced" report every minute, forever (mutation-tested; the test that catches it is the one that runs a SECOND pass after a real sync).
- **Only ever REPLACES an existing entry, matched on `app` + `id`.** Never creates: auto-saving every login the user touches is a different, opt-in feature (`auto_save_unsaved_live_account`). `app` is part of the match because an id is NOT globally unique — both the codex and claude derivations fall back to the EMAIL when the account carries no primary id, so one person signed into both with the same address holds two entries under one id, and an id-only lookup let a codex pass overwrite the claude entry outright, `app` field included. Every other "refresh an existing entry" helper here already matched on both; this one was written without it.
- **The lookup runs on the copy about to be written**, not the one read at the top. The credential read between them can take a hundred milliseconds (that `security(1)` spawn), and a DELETE landing in that window is the one conflict that does not heal: writing the pre-delete copy back resurrects the account and every later pass keeps it alive. **A mutex was tried and REMOVED** — it closed only the microseconds between this read and this write, while the window that matters is the caller's slow work, and a plain `Mutex` is not reentrant, so any future closure reaching back into a store write (`save_*_account` is the obvious slip) would hang with no panic and no log. Deciding inside is what does the work.
- **Boundaries with the flows that own the same files**: gated on `login_in_progress` (a login flow blanks/replaces the credential — same guard the quota path uses) and on `config::disabled_sources` (a disabled tool is absent from every surface that would show this, and on macOS Claude the work is a `security(1)` spawn per minute — same rule `trigger_quota_refresh` applies). Provider switching's `auth_mode`/`OPENAI_API_KEY` are stripped at `read_codex_live`'s single point, so an activate/deactivate produces no diff. **Grok's auth.json is read WITHOUT taking grok's `auth.json.lock`** — acquiring stamps `PID:TIMESTAMP` into the lock file, which lives in the recursively-watched grok home, so every pass would emit a filesystem event and trigger a full session rescan; a torn read is caught by the parse instead.
- **A credential that cannot be READ is not a failure to report.** The watcher fires ON the write, so a read landing mid-write parses as garbage — reporting it would paint the footer red for a CLI that is working fine, and a genuinely corrupt file would repaint it every minute forever. Those return `Ok(false)` (logged, silent); only a failure to WRITE the store is reported. The user still learns about a broken credential: the quota card reads the same file and surfaces `ParseError` in its own right.
- **Reporting** — `ACCOUNTS_CHANGED_EVENT` (`termory:accounts-changed`, mirrored in src/constants.ts) carries `AccountSyncEvent { app, ok, error? }`, emitted ONLY on the two outcomes worth reporting; a pass that changed nothing emits nothing, because only a real update counts as having synced. `OfficialAccountsSection` re-reads on `ok` only (a failed pass changed nothing, and re-reading would risk replacing good rows with an error) and **silently** — `reload(silent)`'s one silent caller, since the footer already reports and a toast from a refresh nobody asked for announces something the user cannot act on. `App.tsx` feeds the outcome to `FreshnessFooter`'s `accountSync` prop, which renders it through the footer's EXISTING states and wording (success pulse, failure state + its tooltip; no new i18n key). It deliberately does NOT move `lastSyncedAt` — that clock dates the session scan, driven by the watcher, and an account sync neither re-scans sessions nor shares its timing.
- Tests: `quota::tests::credential_cli_for_path_matches_claudes_keychain_signal_files` (the lock routes to Claude; `.claude.json` explicitly does NOT; a lock elsewhere does not either), `watcher::tests::{the_login_signal_is_recognized_and_kept_out_of_the_credential_route, credential_signal_parents_dedupes_to_the_existing_home, home_level_signal_events_do_not_trigger_a_session_rescan}`, `accounts::tests::auto_sync_*` (follows the credential / leaves other accounts alone / never creates / does not resurrect a deleted account / does not touch another CLI's entry sharing the id / claude plan upgrade / Max multiplier change / stays quiet on an unparseable credential), `synced_at_is_the_auto_syncs_own_field_and_saved_at_is_not`, `entry_comparison_ignores_bookkeeping_and_key_order`, `list_position_is_first_saved_order_and_updates_do_not_move_rows`; frontend `FreshnessFooter.test.tsx` (success pulse / failure state / quiet when no new outcome) + `OfficialAccountsSection.test.tsx` (no toast on an auto-sync failure, the user-triggered contrast, no re-read on a failed pass, other-CLI events ignored). **Two of these tests were fake when first written and were caught by mutation, not by running them** — the delete case was short-circuited by the "nothing saved for this CLI" gate, and the id-collision case put the codex entry first so a first-match lookup landed correctly anyway. Mutate before believing a negative test.

**`rateLimitTier` → the Max multiplier in the plan label.** `subscriptionType` alone cannot tell Max 5x from Max 20x (both are `"max"`), and the multiplier sits beside it in the same credential block as `rateLimitTier` (auth.ts:1227, written by the same profile fetch). `quota::claude_plan_label(subscriptionType, rateLimitTier)` folds them into `"Max 20x"`. **Only Max takes a multiplier** — Claude Code itself never reads the tier alone (`isMax && rateLimitTier == …` at upgrade.tsx:25, rate-limit-options.tsx:52, planModeV2.ts:18), and the reason shows in `isTeamPremiumSubscriber` (auth.ts:1687), where `default_claude_max_5x` appears under a **team** subscription: the string does not name a Max level on its own, so appending it elsewhere invents a "Team 5x" that means something else. Unrecognized/absent tiers fall back to the bare plan — the value set is not closed (only `default_claude_max_20x` / `_5x` appear anywhere in Claude's source), so a new one must degrade to today's correct-but-vaguer label, never surface a raw identifier. **Both plan derivations go through it** (`quota.rs`'s credential parse and `accounts.rs`'s `claude_live_from`), or one account would show `Max` on the quota card and `Max 20x` on its account row. No new wire field: only the string inside the existing `plan` changed, and nothing compares it (checked). The multiplier rides the payload comparison, so a 5x→20x upgrade — invisible to `subscriptionType` — is caught by the auto-sync.

Gemini returns a DISPLAY-ONLY `current` (`~/.gemini/oauth_creds.json` id_token JWT claims) with an empty `accounts` list. The section renders for `ACCOUNT_SUPPORTED` (ProvidersPage — quota apps + grok); quota props stay gated on `QUOTA_SUPPORTED`. Frontend: `OfficialAccountsSection`'s `isManaged` is codex/claude/grok, and the page's `LOGIN_APPS` record (one entry per CLI whose login Termory can spawn) drives the Add-account button, the shared login dialog and the single `loginApp` lock — replacing what had been three parallel copies of the same state
- `save_account(app)` — snapshot the current live login into `accounts.json` (upsert by `id`)
- `switch_account(id)` — restore a saved snapshot to `auth.json`, always attempt token refresh first; `AuthFailure` (4xx non-429) marks `needsRelogin: true` on the entry. All three managed CLIs behave identically here. **Refused while an add-account flow owns that CLI's credential (LOCKED), at BOTH user entry points** — the IPC (via `accounts::account_cli` to resolve the id's CLI, then `login_in_progress`) and the tray's `spawn_account_switch`. A switch WRITES auth.json, and the flow overwrites it when it ends (`switch_account(prev_id)` on success, `restore_auth` on cancel), so the user's choice was silently undone after a success toast, having spent a token refresh getting there. The tray needs its own check because its row is reachable from the menu bar with the window closed. **The guard must NOT move into `accounts::switch_account`**: the login flow calls that to restore the previous account while still holding its own slot, so a guard inside would block the flow's own cleanup. Frontend greys the button out (`loginInProgress` on the switch row in OfficialAccountsSection) so it is not merely rejected after the confirm dialog. **`delete_account` is deliberately NOT guarded**: it only touches the store, never auth.json, so deleting the account a running flow will restore leaves at worst a live login with no saved entry — surfaced as "current login not saved" with a Save button, recoverable, not a silent loss (a cancel restores from a BYTE snapshot taken before the flow, which does not consult the store)
- `delete_account(id)` — remove a saved snapshot from `accounts.json` (never touches `auth.json`)
- `login_and_save_codex_account()` — snapshot current (via the shared `resnapshot_live_before_login`) → clear auth.json → spawn `codex login` (5 min timeout) → save new login → restore previous; rollback (via `restore_auth`) on any failure including cancel. Uses `tokio::select!` with three arms: child exit / 5-min timeout / cancel signal. A `CodexLoginCancel` Tauri managed state (`Mutex<Option<Arc<Notify>>>`) holds the active notify; the cancel arm calls `stop_codex_login` (NOT `child.kill()` — see below), `restore_auth`, and clears the state.

**Cancelling a codex login must ASK the server to stop, not kill the child (LOCKED).** An npm-installed codex is a node WRAPPER that spawns the real binary (`bin/codex.js:195`), and that wrapper is the only process we hold a handle to. `Child::kill` sends SIGKILL, which is uncatchable, so the wrapper dies before its own `forwardSignal` (codex.js:223) can run and the real binary is re-parented and keeps going. Observed directly: on macOS a cancelled login left pid 91035 with `PPID=1` still holding `127.0.0.1:1455`; on Windows the same cancel left `codex.exe` 9712 (from `%APPDATA%\npm\…\vendor\…`) holding it. **The orphan is not idle** — `curl`ing its `/auth/callback` returned `400 State mismatch`, i.e. a live OAuth server that merely rejected a made-up state. The browser tab is still open with the REAL state, so finishing that login writes credentials after `restore_auth` has already put the previous account back, silently undoing the cancel. Two consecutive runs showed it leaks every time yet the listener count stays at 1 (a later `codex login` clears the stale one through codex's own `send_cancel_request`, server.rs:586) — "doesn't pile up" is not "harmless". `stop_codex_login` therefore sends codex's own `GET /cancel`, which responds and then EXITS (`login/src/server.rs:445`, `HandledRequest::ResponseAndExit`), waits up to 2 s for the child to exit on its own (the wrapper follows the real binary out — codex.js awaits it, then mirrors its exit), and only then falls back to `child.kill()`. Being plain HTTP, the REQUEST is identical everywhere — but do not read that as "no platform differences on this path": what a failed connect COSTS is wildly platform-dependent, and on Windows it silently made every serverless cancel a 2s stall (see the CONNECT-timeout bullet below). Three choices follow codex rather than intuition:
- **Only `DEFAULT_PORT` (1455), never the `FALLBACK_PORT` beside it.** codex's own `/cancel` call is gated on `!using_fallback_port` (server.rs:585). Sweeping both would fire `/cancel` at whatever else occupies a port we did not bind, while our own server on the fallback went untouched.
- **The send result is checked; the RESPONSE is not.** `send_codex_cancel` reports whether it connected and wrote, mirroring codex's `io::Result<()>` (`?` on connect and write, bare `let _ = read(...)` for the reply). The caller needs that much — nothing listening means nothing to wait for, so it should kill immediately instead of sitting out the grace period — but a status line would only be a second, weaker signal that can contradict the real one.
- **The CONNECT timeout is 300ms, separate from the 2s reply timeout — and that split is a Windows fix.** Unix answers a connect to a closed loopback port with an instant ECONNREFUSED, so "nothing is listening" costs nothing there and one 2s constant for both was invisible. **Windows sends no RST**: measured on real Win11 hardware, a connect to ANY closed loopback port — 1455 included — gets no answer at all and sits out the full timeout. So every cancel with no server on 1455 stalled the user for 2s before the fallback kill even started, reintroducing one layer down exactly the cost `CANCEL_GRACE` was split out to remove. **No known everyday trigger — and two guesses at one have already been wrong.** The fallback port is not it: `bind_server` (server.rs:562-610) answers an occupied 1455 by sending it `/cancel` and retrying 10x at 200ms, dropping to 1457 only if something held the port through all 2s without answering — and the usual occupant, a codex orphan, is what codex clears there and what `process.rs` now stops leaking. "Cancelled before the server bound" is not it either: `run_login_server` binds at server.rs:149 and only opens the browser at :172, so by the time focus leaves Termory the port is listening, and coming back to click Cancel always connects. What is left is a few hundred ms between the click and the browser, which needs the user to regret the action they just took. So this is defence in depth, not a fix for something users hit: it costs nothing on the success path, and when a connect does fail for whatever reason it saves 1.7s. **Do not invent a third scenario to make it sound weightier** — the measured Windows behaviour, and the fact that connecting and waiting for a reply are different waits, are reason enough. 300ms is ~1000x what a loopback connect needs (no DNS, no network, a kernel operation) and overshooting degrades gracefully — a missed `/cancel` falls through to `terminate`, which kills the job anyway.
- **The verdict is whether the CHILD EXITS**, which the 2 s wait measures; not a response code.
- **The fallback is no longer wrapper-only** (changed when `process.rs` landed). It used to be a bare `child.kill()` — exactly the signal that leaves the grandchild running — and this bullet used to say so. It is now `process::Managed::terminate`, which signals the whole process group / Job Object, so the orphaned-server case is covered on the fallback path too. `/cancel` is still tried FIRST and stays the preferred path: a server that shuts itself down releases its port properly and lets the wrapper mirror its exit, which no signal can do. `restore_auth` runs afterwards either way.

**Scope: codex only, and here is exactly what was and wasn't checked.** The other LOGIN flows were run and observed: `claude auth login` is a single process with no children (pid 45762, listening on a random port — `listen(port ?? 0)`, auth-code-listener.ts:46) and cancels clean; `grok login` likewise, under BOTH its official installer and `npm install -g @xai-official/grok` — that package's postinstall drops the real binary into `$GROK_HOME/bin`, which precedes the npm bin dir on the PATH its installer configures, so the node trampoline it also ships is not what runs (invoke the trampoline by its explicit path and grok IS two processes, but nothing does that in practice). gemini has no login subcommand at all. **The upgrade and version-probe kill paths were NOT measured either way** — reaching them needs a 600 s / 5 s timeout that has never been observed — so the honest statement is "no evidence of a leak there", not "confirmed clean". A `sleep &`-style harness, which manufactures the grandchild it then detects, proves nothing about whether a real CLI has one.

**This section's scope note is now historical.** It used to end by warning against generalising the codex finding into process-group kills elsewhere — correct while each call site hand-rolled its own kill, since that meant per-site `unsafe` and per-site risk for an unmeasured problem. `process.rs` changed the trade: the group container is applied once, at one seam, for every managed child, so the upgrade and version-probe paths get it for free rather than by extrapolation. What remains true is the epistemics — those two paths are still UNMEASURED against a real CLI, and the module covering them is not evidence that they ever leaked.

Tests (`accounts::tests`, ordinary — they never launch codex, open a browser, or touch the real 1455; a stub `TcpListener` on port 0 plays the login server, which is why `send_codex_cancel` / `stop_codex_login` take a `port` argument at all): `send_codex_cancel_issues_the_cancel_request` pins the request line to `GET /cancel HTTP/1.1` (a wrong verb or path gets a 404 from the real server and cancels nothing); `send_codex_cancel_reports_false_when_nothing_listens` asserts the verdict AND that reaching it took under a second, since a verdict costing more than the wait it skips is worthless (this is the one that catches the Windows no-RST case above); `stop_codex_login_lets_the_child_exit_on_its_own` asserts a SUCCESSFUL exit status, which is what distinguishes "exited by itself" from "was killed"; `stop_codex_login_kills_at_once_when_nothing_answers` asserts both the kill AND that it took well under the 2 s grace period — deleting the `sent &&` short-circuit turns that one red (verified by mutation). Both `stop_codex_login` cases have `_on_windows` mirrors driving `cmd.exe` children: the fallback leg is `Managed::terminate`, whose Windows implementation is a Job Object rather than a process group, and the orphan this whole path exists to prevent was measured on Windows too. The frontend shows a Cancel button both in the URL dialog and on the Official card while login is in progress.
- `cancel_codex_login()` — fires the `Arc<Notify>` stored in `CodexLoginCancel`; the `login_and_save_codex_account` select! cancel arm restores auth.json and returns `Err("Login cancelled")`; frontend `finally` block refreshes the account list so the restored account shows as active immediately. **The tray is rebuilt by the LOGIN ipc, on BOTH outcomes, and never by the cancel ipc (LOCKED — all three CLIs).** Firing the cancel is not the rollback: the flow still has to stop the child (HTTP `/cancel` plus up to 2s waiting for it to exit) before `restore_auth` runs, so a rebuild in the cancel ipc reads the auth.json the flow blanked at the START — every saved account then compares as inactive and the tray's account checkmark disappears. Nothing corrected it either, because the login ipc's own rebuild sat behind a `?` and a cancel returns `Err`, so it survived until the app restarted (reported bug). The same `?` swallowed the rebuild after EVERY failure path — the 5-minute timeout and a failed save also roll auth.json back. So the login ipc now binds the result, rebuilds, and returns it. Not unit-testable: it is IPC-layer timing over a real `AppHandle` and tray
- `mark_account_relogin(id, needed)` — set or clear `needsRelogin` on a saved entry; frontend calls with `true` on switch failure, cleared automatically by `save_account` upsert (Claude and Grok set it BACKEND-side instead — see their sections)
- `login_and_save_grok_account()` / `cancel_grok_login()` — the same flow shape via `grok login --device-auth`, with a `GrokLoginCancel` managed state. Emits `grok:login-url` plus `grok:login-code` (the device-flow user code — the other two CLIs have no code, so the shared dialog renders that row only when it arrives)

Every one of these mutating commands takes the `AppHandle` and calls `tray::rebuild_menu` after its write — the tray lists the saved logins under each CLI's Official row, so any store or live-login change moves a row, its checkmark, or its ⚠ suffix. `accounts::tray_accounts(cli)` builds those rows (Codex + Claude + Grok; `[]` elsewhere) and the pre-overwrite snapshot is split across TWO helpers that answer different questions, and conflating them was a real bug:

- **`resnapshot_live_before_login(app)` — UNCONDITIONAL**, used by all three login flows. A login DESTROYS the outgoing credential (Claude's `performLogout` wipes local storage; a grok login overwrites the one shared scope entry; the codex flow blanks auth.json itself), and all three CLIs rotate their refresh token — so the copy on disk is the only one that still works, and an "already saved, nothing to do" shortcut leaves the restore at the end of the flow reaching for a rotated-away token. **`login_and_save_codex_account` really did take that shortcut until 2026-07-31**: the rotation hazard was found and fixed in `switch_codex` (commit `d318d55`) but never carried back to the login path, so adding a second Codex account could flag the FIRST one `needsRelogin`. Pinned by `resnapshot_before_login_refreshes_an_already_saved_codex_entry`.
- **`accounts::auto_save_unsaved_live_account(app)` — ONLY-IF-MISSING**, used by the tray's account switch. There the question is "would this unattended switch lose a login the user never saved?" (the page asks in a confirm dialog; a native menu row has nowhere to ask), and freshness is already handled by `switch_*`'s own outgoing re-snapshot — so an extra write on every menu-driven switch buys nothing. Pinned by `auto_save_stays_only_if_missing`.

**App-local KV stores** (all `chmod 0600` on Unix)
- `read_app_config` / `write_app_config` — `~/.termory/config.json` (UI prefs)
- `read_app_providers` / `write_app_providers` — `~/.termory/providers.json` unified `providers` array, entries with `kind != "gateway"` (per-CLI provider library, contains API keys)
- `read_app_gateways` / `write_app_gateways` — same `~/.termory/providers.json` unified `providers` array, entries with `kind == "gateway"` (gateway entries with `bindings`)
- `read_app_favorites` / `write_app_favorites` — `~/.termory/favorites.json` (saved message snapshots, may contain PII / pasted secrets)

## Project Commands

- Package manager: npm (`package-lock.json` is present).
- Web dev server: `npm run dev`
- Tauri dev app: `npm run tauri:dev`
- Frontend production build: `npm run build`
- Tauri bundle build: `npm run tauri:build`
- Rust tests: `cargo test --manifest-path src-tauri/Cargo.toml --lib`
- Rust format: `cd src-tauri && cargo fmt`

The Tauri binary is renamed via `[[bin]] name = "Termory"` in `src-tauri/Cargo.toml` plus `mainBinaryName: "Termory"` in `tauri.conf.json`, so the macOS menu bar shows "Termory" rather than the lowercase Cargo package name.

## Release & Updater

CI: `.github/workflows/test.yml` runs on every push/PR (docs-only changes — `**.md`, `docs/**` — are paths-ignore'd) — vitest + `npm run build` (type-check) on ubuntu, and `cargo test --lib` on an ubuntu + macos + **windows** matrix (the Windows leg is what actually executes the `#[cfg(windows)]`-gated tests, e.g. `windows_open_command`/`executable_candidates`). `tauri::generate_context!` needs `../dist` to exist at compile time, so the rust job stubs `dist/index.html` first. **test.yml is ALSO the release gate:** it exposes `workflow_call`, and `release.yml`'s first job is `test: uses: ./.github/workflows/test.yml` with `create-release` (hence build + publish) `needs: test` — so a `v*` tag runs the full suite FIRST and only builds/publishes when it's green. A red test aborts before any draft is created, so a broken commit can never ship a release (single source of truth — the suite is not duplicated in release.yml).

GitHub Actions workflow at `.github/workflows/release.yml` is triggered when a `v*` tag is pushed (e.g. `git tag v0.2.0 && git push --tags`) or manually via `workflow_dispatch`. It uses `tauri-apps/tauri-action@v0` and builds installers for:

- macOS Apple Silicon (`aarch64-apple-darwin`)
- macOS Intel (`x86_64-apple-darwin`)
- Linux x86_64 (`x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu`) — with apt deps for webkit2gtk-4.1, soup-3.0, javascriptcoregtk-4.1, etc.
- Windows x86_64 (`x86_64-pc-windows-msvc`)

A GitHub Release is created with the platform installers attached plus `latest.json` (the updater manifest), and is published AUTOMATICALLY once all four platform builds succeed — it stays a draft only if one of them fails.

### In-app updater (`tauri-plugin-updater`)

- Plugin registered in `lib.rs`: `tauri_plugin_updater::Builder::new().build()` + `tauri_plugin_process::init()` for `relaunch()`.
- `tauri.conf.json` declares `bundle.createUpdaterArtifacts: true` and `plugins.updater.endpoints` pointing at `https://github.com/chats-is/termory/releases/latest/download/latest.json`.
- `capabilities/default.json` grants `updater:default` + `process:default`.
- Frontend: `Settings` page exposes "Check for updates" → `@tauri-apps/plugin-updater::check()` → "Download and install" → `update.downloadAndInstall()` → `@tauri-apps/plugin-process::relaunch()`.

### One-time signing key setup (DONE as of v0.2.6)

The updater only installs artifacts whose signature matches a pubkey baked into the binary. Without a keypair, in-app updates won't work (but the GitHub Actions builds still produce installers — users can download manually).

**Status: configured.** The keypair lives at `~/.tauri/termory.key` (local, password-protected — back it up; losing it means no more signed updates for existing users, and the locked bundle id makes that unrecoverable). The pubkey is baked into `tauri.conf.json` `plugins.updater.pubkey`, `bundle.createUpdaterArtifacts` is `true`, and the GitHub repo secrets `TAURI_SIGNING_PRIVATE_KEY` + `TAURI_SIGNING_PRIVATE_KEY_PASSWORD` are set. Signed updater artifacts (`.sig` + `latest.json`) ship from v0.2.6 on, so **auto-update works only for users already on ≥0.2.6** — earlier installs (no baked pubkey) must download the new version manually once. CI still creates a **draft** release; publish it (un-draft) so `…/releases/latest/download/latest.json` resolves.

The original setup steps, kept for reference / re-keying:

```sh
# Generates ~/.tauri/termory.key (private, password-protected) + termory.key.pub
npx @tauri-apps/cli signer generate -w ~/.tauri/termory.key
```

Then:

1. Paste the contents of `~/.tauri/termory.key.pub` into `tauri.conf.json` `plugins.updater.pubkey`.
2. Add GitHub repo secrets:
   - `TAURI_SIGNING_PRIVATE_KEY` = contents of `~/.tauri/termory.key` (the private file)
   - `TAURI_SIGNING_PRIVATE_KEY_PASSWORD` = the password you set during generation
3. Tag and push: `git tag v0.2.0 && git push --tags` — the workflow builds, signs, and **publishes the release automatically**. The draft is only an intermediate state: a `publish` job (`needs: [create-release, build]`) flips it to public once every platform build has uploaded, so `…/releases/latest/download/latest.json` resolves for the in-app updater. Only a FAILED matrix job leaves it a draft. **Pushing a tag is therefore not reversible in practice** — users' auto-updaters can pick it up within minutes.

**Never choose a MAJOR or MINOR bump on your own (LOCKED).** Default to a PATCH bump (`1.3.0` → `1.3.1`) and, when anything about the release seems to warrant more than that, ASK — do not decide from the diff. "It adds a user-visible feature, so semver says MINOR" is exactly the reasoning that has been overruled twice: this project's version line is a product decision, not a mechanical read of what changed. Tagging is effectively irreversible (see the auto-publish note below), so the cost of asking is one message and the cost of guessing is a public release under the wrong number.

**Version bump (LOCKED) — before tagging, bump the `version` in ALL FIVE files to the SAME value so every layer self-reports correctly:**

1. `package.json`
2. `package-lock.json` (the two `"version"` entries at the top — the root `version` AND `packages[""].version`; run `npm install` to sync it rather than hand-editing)
3. `src-tauri/Cargo.toml`
4. `src-tauri/Cargo.lock` (run `cargo update -p termory --manifest-path src-tauri/Cargo.toml` to sync it)
5. `src-tauri/tauri.conf.json`

Skipping `package.json` / `package-lock.json` leaves the npm layer (dev-server banner, any `npm`-read version) stale, and skipping the lockfiles leaves a dirty tree / CI drift. Verify with `grep -rn '"version"' package.json src-tauri/tauri.conf.json | ...` before committing.

macOS bundle identifier: `is.chats.termory` (reverse DNS of the `chats.is` domain the project ships under). Do NOT change this after a public release — macOS treats a different identifier as a different app, so existing user data and the Tauri updater would break.

The repo also contains `.audit-sources/` (gitignored) with shallow clones of `openai/codex`, `google-gemini/gemini-cli`, `sst/opencode`, `xai-org/grok-build`, `videcoding/cli` (legacy Claude Code reference), and `farion1231/cc-switch` (reference for provider-switcher behavior). This is the source-of-truth for path/behavior verification when official docs disagree with implementation — grep here instead of WebFetching docs.

## Upstream References

Use upstream implementations as the reference for history data and message preview behavior:

- Codex official source: https://github.com/openai/codex
- Claude Code referenced CLI implementation: https://github.com/videcoding/cli — **STALE and incomplete: it stopped in April 2026 and has none of the tools added since** (`ReportFindings`, `Artifact`, `Monitor`, `SendMessage`, …). Claude Code is closed source, so the authoritative reference for anything it lacks is **the shipped binary** — a Bun single-file executable with its JS strings in the clear. Locate a tool by its definition anchor and read the real values:
  ```sh
  python3 - <<'EOF'
  import mmap; m=mmap.mmap(open('/Users/…/.local/bin/claude','rb').fileno(),0,access=mmap.ACCESS_READ)
  i=m.find(b'userFacingName(){return"Monitor"}'); print(m[i:i+400].decode('utf-8','replace'))
  EOF
  ```
  Grep for `"ToolName"` first to find the block, then read `userFacingName` / `renderToolUseMessage` verbatim — **do NOT assume the verb equals the tool name** (`ReportFindings` renders as `Code review`), and check whether `renderToolUseMessage` exists at all, since its absence means the TUI shows the verb with no arguments. Record the binary version next to anything recovered this way (these came from **2.1.226**).
- Gemini CLI official source: https://github.com/google-gemini/gemini-cli
- OpenCode official source: https://github.com/anomalyco/opencode
- Grok Build official source: https://github.com/xai-org/grok-build (Rust; the token-usage ledgers are `crates/codegen/xai-chat-state/src/usage.rs` + `.../actor/state.rs`, the ACP wire shapes `crates/codegen/xai-grok-shell/src/extensions/notification.rs`)

For Memory paths:

- Claude Code memory: https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory
- Codex AGENTS.md guide: https://developers.openai.com/codex/guides/agents-md
- Codex memories: https://developers.openai.com/codex/memories
- Gemini GEMINI.md: https://github.com/google-gemini/gemini-cli/blob/main/docs/cli/gemini-md.md
- Gemini memory tool: https://github.com/google-gemini/gemini-cli/blob/main/docs/tools/memory.md
- OpenCode rules: https://opencode.ai/docs/rules/

For Skills paths:

- Codex skills docs: https://developers.openai.com/codex/skills
- Gemini CLI skills docs: https://github.com/google-gemini/gemini-cli/blob/main/docs/cli/skills.md
- OpenCode skills docs: https://opencode.ai/docs/skills/

For TUI tool-message rendering (every Termory branch cites a line here):

- Codex exec/shell render: `.audit-sources/codex/codex-rs/tui/src/exec_cell/render.rs`, bash highlight alias at `codex-rs/tui/src/render/highlight.rs:533`
- Claude tool-use wrapper: `.audit-sources/claude-code/src/components/messages/AssistantToolUseMessage.tsx:152` (assembles `<bold>{userFacingName}</bold>({renderToolUseMessage})`); per-tool: `src/tools/<Tool>/UI.tsx` (`userFacingName` + `renderToolUseMessage`)
- Gemini ToolInfo render: `.audit-sources/gemini-cli/packages/cli/src/ui/components/messages/ToolShared.tsx:202`; type at `packages/cli/src/ui/types.ts:119` `IndividualToolCallDisplay`
- OpenCode tool components: `.audit-sources/opencode/packages/tui/src/routes/session/index.tsx` — the per-tool renderers (now named functions: `Shell`/`Glob`/`Read`/`Grep`/`WebFetch`/`WebSearch`/`Write`/`Edit`/`ApplyPatch`/`TodoWrite`/`Question`/`Skill`/`Task`/`Execute`/`GenericTool`) + the `BlockTool` / `InlineTool` helpers. **OpenCode 1.17.x extracted the whole TUI into the `packages/tui` package** (the old `…/feature-plugins/system/session-v2.tsx` is gone). Citations in the OpenCode verb-mapping section have been re-verified against 1.18.15; message-type DATA now lives in `packages/tui/src/context/data.tsx`. See the audit-version note under "Per-platform verb mapping → OpenCode".

When behavior differs by version, match the locally installed or explicitly requested target version and cover it with a focused test. Tool-message rendering should reference the TUI source files above, not the doc sites — docs lag behind the actual UI for many of these tools.

## Current Data Sources

### Sessions

- Codex
  - List: `~/.codex/state_5.sqlite`, table `threads`, rows where `archived = 0` AND `preview <> ''` AND `source IN ('cli', 'vscode', 'atlas', 'chatgpt')`. The four sources match `INTERACTIVE_SESSION_SOURCES` in `codex-rs/rollout/src/lib.rs`; the `preview <> ''` clause matches `push_thread_filters` in `codex-rs/state/src/runtime/threads.rs`. Same filter is applied when loading a single thread by id.
  - Messages: each selected thread's `threads.rollout_path` JSONL file.
- Claude Code
  - List: `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR/projects/**/*.jsonl` when `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` is set, otherwise `~/.claude/projects/**/*.jsonl`. Filename must be a UUID (`is_uuid_like`), first line must NOT contain `"isSidechain":true`, and the session must have at least one of customTitle/aiTitle/lastPrompt/summary/firstPrompt — same filter as videcoding/cli `parseSessionInfoFromLite`.
  - Messages: the selected project JSONL file.
- Gemini CLI
  - List: `~/.gemini/tmp/*/chats/session-*.jsonl` and `~/.gemini/tmp/*/chats/session-*.json`. Sessions must have a non-empty `sessionId`, **`hasResumableContent`**, and `kind !== 'subagent'`. `hasResumableContent` mirrors `getAllSessionFiles` gating on `isResumableMessageRecord` (`sessionUtils.ts:284` + `chatRecordingService.ts:107`): a `user` turn counts unless its content is *ignored* (`isIgnoredUserContent`, `sessionUtils.ts:97` — empty / `/` slash command / `?` shell passthrough / `<session_context>` / `<hook_context>`), a `gemini` turn counts when it has text, tool calls, or thoughts. This REPLACED the earlier "any user/gemini message" rule (the CLI tightened the filter to skip startup-only / command-only / context-only shells; helpers `gemini_is_resumable_message` / `gemini_is_ignored_user_content`, test `gemini_list_filter_gates_on_resumable_content`). The `~/.gemini/tmp/<id>/` dir id is now a **slug** (was a sha256 hash pre-v0.50; managed by `ProjectRegistry` + `~/.gemini/projects.json`, old hash dirs auto-migrated) — but Termory reads the `.project_root` marker for the cwd and never decodes the id, so hash→slug is transparent. When `startTime`/`lastUpdated` are missing on the record, Termory falls back to the file's mtime (then to `Utc::now()`) — also mirrors `getAllSessionFiles`.
  - Project path: sibling/related `.project_root` file under the Gemini temp project directory.
  - Messages: the selected session JSONL/JSON chat file.
- OpenCode
  - List: `~/.local/share/opencode/opencode.db`, table `session`, rows where `parent_id IS NULL` and `time_archived IS NULL`, ordered by `time_updated DESC, id DESC`. Mirrors `listByProject` in `packages/opencode/src/session/session.ts`.
  - Messages: `~/.local/share/opencode/opencode.db`, tables `message` and `part`; `session_message` is only a fallback when `message`/`part` are unavailable (a real compat path for older databases — `session_message` is otherwise the projections table per `projectors-next.ts`).
  - Compatibility storage: JSON files under `~/.local/share/opencode/**/storage`; use only for older/alternate local layouts and never before the current SQLite path.

- Grok Build (xAI)
  - Grok Build **is open source** (`xai-org/grok-build`, cloned to `.audit-sources/grok-build`) — cite it like the other CLIs. (It was closed-source when Termory's grok support was first written, so older notes here said the on-disk layout was the only reference; the layout survey still holds, re-verified on real data at grok 0.2.99.)
  - List: `$GROK_HOME`-aware (`providers::grok_home_dir`, default `~/.grok`) — `<grok-home>/sessions/<percent-encoded-cwd>/<uuid>/summary.json` — read VERBATIM (`info.id`, `info.cwd`, `created_at`/`updated_at`, `num_chat_messages`, `current_model_id`). **Title = official `display_title()`** (persistence.rs:958): `generated_title` (LLM/manual `/rename`, trimmed non-empty) preferred over `session_summary`. **Hidden sessions are skipped** per the official `is_hidden()` (persistence.rs:949): explicit `hidden` bool wins, default = `session_kind` starts with `"subagent"` — grok's sidechain analog, same list-time rule as Claude's isSidechain. The encoded-cwd dir name is percent-encoding for short cwds but `{slug}-{blake3_16}` + a `.cwd` metadata file when the encoded form exceeds 255 bytes (paths.rs `encode_cwd_dirname`) — Termory never decodes dir names (reads `info.cwd`), so both encodings just work. A session dir is valid iff it has `chat_history.jsonl` or `updates.jsonl`. **ALL empty sessions are DROPPED** (grok opens a NEW session dir on every CLI entry, so blank never-chatted shells abound — a history browser has nothing to show for one, and a cwd containing only empty shells would otherwise surface as a noise project). Implemented as a post-scan `scanned.retain(|g| !g.empty)` in `scan_grok_sessions_in` over `GrokScanned { session, empty }`. **Emptiness is decided in three tiers** (`grok_scanned_from_summary`), so the common cases never read a file: `num_messages == 0` → empty; else a non-empty title (which already falls back to `session_summary`) → real, since grok titles off the first exchange; else stream `chat_history.jsonl` via `grok_chat_has_real_user_turn`. **`num_messages == 0` alone is NOT sufficient** — that was the rule until 2026-08-01 and it leaked. It counts the turns grok INJECTS on entry, and how many exist depends on the DIRECTORY: a bare cwd gets none (so a blank shell really does read 0), but one with project-instruction and skill files gets several. Observed on a real never-chatted session in this repo: `num_messages == 3` (`<user_info>` + two `<system-reminder>`s), i.e. the filter failed exactly where a user is most likely to notice. A slash command does not rescue it either — `/status` and its reply live only in `updates.jsonl`, never in chat_history, and `turn_completed` fires for them, so neither is the signal. The signal that holds is the one the DISPLAY layer already applies: a `user` line is the user's own unless it carries a `synthetic_reason` (conversation.rs:77) **or** unwraps to nothing but envelopes (`grok_user_display_text`'s `injected`). BOTH halves are load-bearing and separately mutation-tested — plain-text synthetics (`auto_continue`) carry no envelope, so the envelope check alone would let a shell through. ONE definition shared with the renderer: if they disagreed, a session would be listed whose every message renders as "system". (History: content-sniffing `grok_chat_has_content` → keep-one-per-cwd → `num_messages` → this. `num_messages` was adopted as "the reliable signal"; it is not.) Tests: `grok_scan_drops_a_shell_whose_only_turns_were_injected` (built from the real leaked session), `grok_scan_keeps_a_session_with_one_real_turn_and_no_title`, `grok_scan_trusts_a_title_without_reading_the_transcript`. Loading a single empty session by id still works — the filter is list-only. **This DIVERGES from grok on purpose** (user decision 2026-07-24, reversing the earlier keep-one-per-cwd rule): grok's own `sessions list` / picker DOES surface one empty per cwd (its internal `dedup_empty_sessions`, session/merge.rs — a "resume most recent" affordance), but that's a picker concern, not history. (History: an even earlier content-sniffing `grok_chat_has_content` hid all empties → replaced 2026-07 by keep-one-per-cwd → now back to dropping all, this time on the reliable `num_messages` signal.) Sibling noise skipped: `session_search.sqlite`, per-cwd `prompt_history.jsonl`, `.lock` files.
  - Messages — grok has TWO on-disk formats; `parse_grok_session` dispatches by the scanned file's name. **CURRENT: `chat_history.jsonl`** (`{type, content}` per line — written by the interactive TUI, present in EVERY session, so it's preferred). `parse_grok_chat_history`: `system` → `*[System prompt]*` notice; `user` → the TUI's display form via `grok_user_display_text`: the LLM-prompt envelopes are never shown raw — `<user_query>` unwraps to the message text (the pager's own extractor, app/effects/helpers.rs:376), every other envelope (`<user_info>` preamble — the "marker-less non-synthetic" item conversation.rs:2513, `<system-reminder>`, `<skill_information>`, …) becomes an italic `*[tag]*` notice (never-hide rule), and a synthetic turn (`synthetic_reason`, conversation.rs:77) or all-envelope message renders with the SYSTEM role like Codex `<environment_context>` / Gemini `<session_context>` (content is a string or `[{type:text,text}]`, flattened by `grok_chat_content_text`); `reasoning` → `summary[].text` as a `> *…*` blockquote; `assistant` → text + one `**{Label}**(arg)` card per `tool_calls[]` entry (cards via the shared `grok_tool_card(name, input)` — used by BOTH formats. **Verbs are the pager's per-block TUI headers** (xai-grok-pager/src/scrollback/blocks/tool/ + acp/tracker.rs `tool_call_to_block`), NOT the `_meta`/taxonomy `presentation_name` — the pager never renders that label in the transcript: read_file→**Read** (+ ` ({start}-{end})` range from offset/limit, tracker.rs:1465), search_replace/edit→**Edit** + a ```diff fence from old_string/new_string (the TUI shows hunks, edit.rs), write→**Creating** (tracker.rs:1530) + all-insert diff, run_terminal_command/bash→**Run** with the DESCRIPTION as title and the command as a secondary `$ …` fence when a description exists (execute.rs:353, default Label style config.rs:699), grep/glob→**Search** with `"{pattern}"[ in {glob}][ in {path}]` (trivial pattern promotes the glob, search.rs:225-280), list_dir→**List** (list_dir.rs:116), **web_fetch→Fetch** (web_fetch.rs:128 — the taxonomy says "Web Fetch" but the TUI block says Fetch), web_search→**Web Search**, memory_search→**Memory Search**, skill→**Skill**; block-less tools (plan/ask-user/task/lsp/background/memory_get/…) keep their taxonomy title, which is what OtherToolCallBlock shows (tracker.rs:1830); unknown names fall back to the raw name; `arguments` is a JSON-encoded string); `tool_result` folds its `content` (4-backtick fence) into the matching card by `tool_call_id`, and its `images[]` (`ToolResultItem.images` — e.g. read_file on an image) append `*Image*`/`*Image: url*` notices into the fold; `backend_tool_call` (SERVER-side tools — `kind.tool_type` = web_search / x_search / code_interpreter, conversation.rs:44) renders per tracker.rs:1534-1670: **Web Search** with per-action detail (search→query, open_page→`open {url}`, find→`find "{pattern}"`) + `action.sources` as a numbered `1. {url}` citations fence (tracker.rs:1641), **X Search** as `{keyword|semantic|users|thread}({query})` (tracker.rs:1573-1590), **Code Interpreter** with a code snippet (fields are OpenAI Responses API items — `rs = async_openai::types::responses`); user `content` parts are `{type:text}`/`{type:image,url}` (`ContentPart`) — image parts surface as `*Image: url*` notices (data URIs → bare `*Image*`). Test: `grok_parse_chat_history_new_format`. **LEGACY (fallback): `updates.jsonl`** — an ACP (Agent Client Protocol) `session/update` stream, still written by headless `grok -p`; used only when a session lacks chat_history.jsonl. `parse_grok_session` (the ACP path): streaming `user/agent_message_chunk` + `agent_thought_chunk` events merge consecutive same-type chunks into one message (thought → unified `> *…*` blockquote); `tool_call` opens the SAME `grok_tool_card` card as chat_history (TUI verbs — the pager renders per-block headers from the tool NAME, not the `_meta["x.ai/tool"].label`, which is the cross-harness taxonomy title); the recorded `_meta` label (JSON-pointer `~1` escape for the literal `x.ai/tool` key) is used only for names outside the table — it's what OtherToolCallBlock would show; the terminal `tool_call_update` (`completed`/`failed`) finishes the card with `⏺`/`✗` + a 4-backtick output fence (`grok_output_text` unwraps the `{type, <Variant>: {content}}` envelope); `plan` → GFM task list; intermediate tool updates / `retry_state` / `turn_completed` are transport bookkeeping (no content) and are skipped. Resume: `grok --resume <id>` (verified against `grok --help`), wired in terminal.rs + `resumeCommandFor`.
  - Tokens/daily_tokens: extracted by `grok_scan_extract` from **`updates.jsonl`** (the ACP rail) — token usage is NOT in `chat_history.jsonl`. It rides each `turn_completed` update's `usage`; every session with a real turn has one (an empty CLI-entry shell ran no inference, so it has no updates.jsonl at all — which is why the 2-of-20 split looked like "headless only"). Read via `cached_scan_extract` (mtime-cached) off the sibling path, regardless of which file `path` points at. **Usage is PER-TURN, so turns SUM** — verified in the official source: the ACP turn-end path reads `try_get_prompt_usage()` (`session/acp_session_impl/turn.rs`), the `prompt_usage` ledger documented in `xai-chat-state/src/actor/state.rs` as *"Billing for the open prompt (cleared on next prompt)"*, NOT the lifetime `session_usage` sibling; `numTurns` counts main-agent loop ROUNDS within one prompt (same unit as `--max-turns`), so it is NOT a cumulative signal. **Mapping**: grok nests the subsets per the xAI/OpenAI convention — `cachedReadTokens ⊆ inputTokens` ("Full prompt input tokens including cache reads", `extensions/notification.rs`) and `reasoningTokens ⊆ outputTokens` (its `total_tokens() = input + output` adds neither) — while Termory's `TokenStats` wants the four DISJOINT, so both subsets are subtracted out; the four then re-sum to grok's own `totalTokens` exactly (verified on real data: `69373-11264` + `60-31` + `11264` + `31` = `69433`). Do NOT take the four fields verbatim (double-counts). Per-day/hour bucketing uses `params._meta.agentTimestampMs` (epoch ms), falling back to the top-level `timestamp` (epoch SECONDS). NOTE `signals.json` also carries `contextTokensUsed`/`contextWindowTokens` (what the TUI's "System prompt / Messages / Free" bar shows) — that is CONTEXT-WINDOW OCCUPANCY, not consumption, and is deliberately not used. Tests: `grok_scan_extract_sums_per_turn_usage_and_splits_subsets`, `grok_scan_extract_empty_without_turns`.

Read source history in place. Do not modify original history files or databases.

### Memory

Verified against each tool's open-source implementation (not just docs). When docs and source disagree, source is authoritative. See Upstream References for source URLs.

- Claude Code: `~/.claude/projects/<sanitized-canonical-git-root>/memory/**/*.md` (auto-memory per project — `src/memdir/paths.ts` uses `findCanonicalGitRoot` so worktrees of the same repo share one dir), `~/.claude/rules/**/*.md` (global rules), `<cwd>/.claude/rules/**/*.md` (project rules — both recursive, all `.md`)
- Codex: `~/.codex/memories/**/*.md` — `scan_codex_memory` skips the `skills/` subdir for backward compatibility (current Codex source stores skills at `~/.codex/skills/`)
- Grok Build: `<grok-home>/memory/**/*.md` ($GROK_HOME-aware) — `scan_grok_memory`. Cross-session memory as human-readable Markdown (xai-grok-memory storage.rs:24): global files at the root, workspace-scoped files under `{project-slug}-{hash8}/` subdirs (hash not reversible; the file's relative path shows the slug). `derive_memory_project_label` recognizes `<grok-home>/{memory,skills}`.
- Gemini CLI:
  - Global: `~/.gemini/GEMINI.md` (legacy) AND `~/.gemini/MEMORY.md` (modern alias — `getAllGeminiMdFilenames()` returns both)
  - Per-project: `~/.gemini/tmp/<id>/memory/{MEMORY.md preferred, GEMINI.md legacy}` — confirmed at `packages/core/src/config/storage.ts getProjectMemoryDir()` → `getProjectMemoryTempDir() = path.join(globalTempDir, projectIdentifier, 'memory')`. Termory recursively reads .md inside, skipping the `skills/` subdir which is surfaced under Skills.
- Per-project instruction files — scanned at the cwd AND, **only when a `.git` directory exists at or above cwd**, every ancestor up to and including the git root (stopping before `$HOME`):
  - `CLAUDE.md` → tag `claude,opencode` (OpenCode officially falls back to it)
  - `CLAUDE.local.md` → tag `claude`
  - `AGENTS.md` → tag `codex,opencode`
  - `AGENTS.override.md` → tag `codex` (Codex's official override file)
  - `GEMINI.md` → tag `gemini`
  - `MEMORY.md` → tag `gemini`
  - `<cwd>/.claude/CLAUDE.md` → tag `claude` (only at cwd, not at ancestors — `.claude/CLAUDE.md` is a project-root convention)
- Global instruction files:
  - `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` → tag `claude,opencode`
  - `~/.codex/AGENTS.md`, `~/.codex/AGENTS.override.md` → tag `codex`
  - `~/.config/opencode/AGENTS.md` → tag `opencode`

Paths intentionally NOT scanned (no current source reads them):

- `AGENTS.local.md` (any location) — not in any tool's source; Codex uses `AGENTS.override.md`
- `~/.codex/instructions.md` — legacy
- `~/.claude/CLAUDE.local.md` — not documented at user level
- `CONTEXT.md` — OpenCode deprecated, intentionally skipped
- `project_doc_fallback_filenames` from `~/.codex/config.toml` — Termory does not read user config

### Why ancestor walk gates on `.git`

All three open-source tools refuse to ascend without a project-root marker:

- **Codex** (`codex-rs/core/src/agents_md.rs`): `DEFAULT_PROJECT_ROOT_MARKERS = &[".git"]`. The doc-comment on the loader: *"If no marker is found, only the current working directory is considered."*
- **Gemini** (`packages/core/src/utils/memoryDiscovery.ts findProjectRoot`): defaults to `['.git']`. When no marker is found, returns null → caller sets `ceiling = startDir` → `findUpwardGeminiFiles` breaks immediately on the start dir.
- **OpenCode** (`packages/opencode/src/project/project.ts`): `worktree` is resolved via `git rev-parse --git-common-dir`; without git the fallback sets `worktree: sandbox` (= cwd), so `Filesystem.findUp(start=cwd, stop=cwd)` collects only cwd.

Claude Code (the only one NOT gating on `.git` — its `attachments.ts` walks to fs root) is the outlier; for simplicity we apply the stricter (more common) rule.

The implementation lives in `scan_memory`:

1. `push_project_root_instruction_files(cwd, ...)` always runs (cwd-level files including `.claude/CLAUDE.md`).
2. `find_git_root(cwd, home)` walks up looking for `.git`. Returns `Some(dir)` or `None`.
3. If `Some(git_root)` and `git_root != cwd`, walk from `cwd.parent()` up to and including `git_root`, calling `push_ancestor_instruction_files` at each level (omits `.claude/CLAUDE.md`).
4. The final dedup-by-path keeps each file's first label; ancestor files get their own ancestor dir as the project label.

### Skills

Source-verified locations:

| Tool | Global | Project | Tag |
|---|---|---|---|
| Claude Code | `~/.claude/skills/` | `<cwd>/.claude/skills/` | `claude,opencode,grok` (OpenCode officially reads `.claude/skills/`; Grok Build reads `~/.claude/` skills for Claude compatibility — docs.x.ai/build/features/skills-plugins-marketplaces) |
| Codex | `~/.codex/skills/` (NOT `~/.codex/memories/skills/`) | `<cwd>/.codex/skills/` | `codex` |
| Gemini CLI | `~/.gemini/skills/` (`Storage.getUserSkillsDir`) | `~/.gemini/tmp/<id>/memory/skills/` (`Storage.getProjectSkillsMemoryDir`) + `<cwd>/.gemini/skills/` | `gemini` |
| OpenCode | `~/.config/opencode/skills/` | `<cwd>/.opencode/skills/` | `opencode` |
| Grok Build | `<grok-home>/skills/` ($GROK_HOME-aware) | `<cwd>/.grok/skills/` (official `./.grok/skills/`; cwd-level like the other tools) | `grok` (plugin skills dirs + `[skills] paths` extras deliberately not scanned) |
| Tool-neutral | `~/.agents/skills/` (`Storage.getUserAgentSkillsDir`) | `<cwd>/.agents/skills/` | `codex,gemini,opencode,grok` at BOTH levels (grok source: `collect_skill_config_dirs` — `.agents` is in the cwd→git-root walk's dir set AND always added at `~/.agents`; an earlier docs-only survey saw just the user level) |

Implementation notes:

- All skill scanners route through `push_doc_files_recursive(dir, base, project, tag, source="Skill", skip_dirs=&[], out)`.
- `doc_session_from_file(path, project, source)` is shared between Memory and Skill scanners; the `source` field on `AppSession` is `"Memory"` or `"Skill"` accordingly. **The `id` is the file's full path (NOT the filename or frontmatter `name`)** — those collide across projects (every `CLAUDE.md` shares the name "CLAUDE.md"), and the id keys BOTH the backend `SESSION_PATH_INDEX` `(source, id)` AND the frontend `sessionKey`; a non-unique id made every same-named memory/skill load the last-scanned file's content. `title` still uses `name` → filename for display.
- `parse_doc_file(path, source)` handles loading either kind in `get_session`.
- `derive_memory_project_label` recognizes both memory and skill on-disk paths (including `.agents/skills/`) so loading a single file by absolute path produces a sensible project label.
- **Claude `projects/<slug>/memory/` project label — read the real cwd, don't decode the slug.** Claude's project-dir slug is LOSSY: it replaces `/`, `.`, `_`, `-` (every non-alphanumeric char) all with `-`, so `/Users/me/copilot.is`, `…/copilot-is` and `…/copilot/is` collapse to the same `-Users-me-copilot-is` and can't be reversed from the slug alone (a blind `-`→`/` decode would show the label as `is`). So `claude_cwd_from_project_dir(slug_dir)` reads the authoritative `cwd` from the first records of a sibling session JSONL (only the first ~8 lines — these files run tens of MB) — independent of whether the project folder still exists on disk, since the JSONL lives under `~/.claude/projects/`, not in the project. Fallback chain when there's NO session (memory-only orphan): `resolve_dashed_path` (probe the filesystem, merging `-`-joined segments that name a real dir) → naive `decode_claude_project_slug` (`-`→`/`). Don't "simplify" this back to the naive decode — covered by `claude_cwd_from_project_dir_reads_real_cwd_from_session` + `resolve_dashed_path_recovers_hyphenated_directory` tests.

## Current Implementation Notes

- `scan_all_sessions` calls Rust scanning on a blocking worker and returns a `ScanResult { projects, records }` — `projects` (enumerated folders/entities) drive the sidebar; `records` (sessions/memories/skills, distinguished by `source`) are the contents. `applyScanResult` reconciles both against the tombstone sets and sets the `projects` + `sessions` (records) state.
- `load_session` loads one selected record by `source` + `id` (path resolved backend-side, never frontend-supplied).
- **Cross-source record sorting is by PARSED instant, not string compare (LOCKED).** The final merge sort in `scan_sessions` (and the tray's `select_recent_state` recent-session list — the same cross-source case) orders by `record_instant(&updated_at)` (RFC3339 → epoch millis; missing/unparseable → `i64::MIN`, sorts last), NOT `updated_at.cmp`. Different sources emit different UTC offsets (`+00:00` vs `Z`; Codex/OpenCode `normalize_time` historically emitted a LOCAL offset), and mixed-offset RFC3339 strings do NOT order lexicographically — a string `cmp` put an older Codex record above a newer Claude one whenever their wall-clock digits crossed (up to the offset difference, e.g. 8h). `normalize_time` now emits UTC too, so stored timestamps are uniform; display is unaffected either way (the frontend parses with `new Date()`). The WITHIN-source dedup sorts in `scan_claude`/`scan_gemini` stay string-based — safe because a single source's timestamps share one format. `record_instant` is `pub(crate)` in sessions.rs (shared with tray.rs). Tests: `sessions::tests::{record_instant_orders_across_mixed_utc_offsets, normalize_time_from_epoch_emits_utc_not_local_offset}` + `tray::tests::select_recent_state_sorts_mixed_offsets_by_instant`.
- **Scan-time extraction is mtime-cached and single-pass (LOCKED).** The Claude/Codex LIST scans get tokens + model + daily_tokens + **message_count** from ONE streamed pass per session file (`claude_scan_extract` / `codex_scan_extract`, 256 KB `BufReader` — these files run 100+ MB), cached by `(path, mtime)` in `cached_scan_extract` (`SCAN_EXTRACT_CACHE`; stale entries evicted each scan by `evict_stale_scan_cache`). Do NOT reintroduce a separate per-scan full-file parse (the old uncached `estimate_*_message_count` pair pegged one core at 100% whenever any CLI was actively writing — every watcher rescan re-parsed ~800 MB; warm rescan went 2.08 s → 8.5 ms). Counting deliberately reuses `claude_message_from_value` / `codex_message_from_value` (no "cheap predicate" — `claude_display_text`'s Some/None IS the visibility rule, a parallel predicate would drift from the DETAIL count). `parse_claude_lite_session` returns the lean `ClaudeLiteSession` (head/tail metadata only), and the call sites assemble the `AppSession`, so `message_count` is a compile-time-required field rather than a fill-me-in-later 0.
- **The Claude/Codex JSONL reads go through `read_record`, which elides over-long strings (LOCKED).** (NOT *every* JSONL read — `load_gemini_jsonl_conversation`, the grok readers and `claude_cwd_from_project_dir` still use `read_to_string`/`lines()`; their files are small and none has shown this failure.) A JSONL record has no size bound: Codex writes a tool's output verbatim into one record, and a runaway `exec_command` produced a **156 MB single line** on a real machine (a 74 MB `patch_apply_end` sat in the same file; 35 lines over 1 MB across all sources, 274 MB total). `BufRead::lines()` + `from_str::<Value>` + building the formatted text multiplied that ~6x, and macOS's allocator never returned the freed pages, so the process **sat at 913 MB for its whole life** — measured, and measured to be reusable-but-unreturned (allocating 400 MB afterwards grew RSS by zero). Claude's own scan, over 165 MB of transcripts whose lines are all small, peaked at 23 MB: this is a single-RECORD problem, not a data-volume one, and it does NOT get worse with more sessions (158 records serialize to 0.1 MB of IPC; warm rescan is 14 ms).
  - `read_record(reader, &mut buf, max_string_bytes)` reads one newline-terminated record into a **reused** buffer via `fill_buf`/`consume`, tracking string/escape state so it can bulk-copy between delimiters. Records are handed to `serde_json::from_slice`, never `from_str` on a fresh `String`.
  - The LIST scan passes **`SCAN_MAX_STRING_BYTES` (64 KB)**; the DETAIL parsers pass **`NO_STRING_LIMIT`**. That split is the whole point — **the scan never keeps message text** (it reads `type` / `timestamp` / `payload.type` / `usage.*` / `model` and a COUNT), so eliding a string's tail there leaves the record's JSON *structure* untouched and `codex_message_from_value` / `claude_message_from_value` reach identical visible/hidden decisions and an identical count: those key off structure and off exact matches against short constants (`(no content)`, `[Request interrupted by user]`, …), never off length. **Rendering is not affected by any of this** — verified by hashing every message of all 122 Claude/Codex sessions (71 075 messages) before and after: byte-identical, the lone difference being the transcript being written *while the comparison ran*.
  - Startup RSS **913 MB → 30 MB**; cold scan 1.55 s → 1.83 s (the byte-level state machine), warm rescan unchanged at 14 ms.
  - **A cut may only land where it cannot produce invalid JSON — four separate hazards, every one mutation-verified.** The cost of getting any of them wrong is identical and silent: `from_slice` fails, the record is `continue`d, and its message count and tokens vanish from the scan. (1) **UTF-8 boundary** — a split multi-byte char is invalid UTF-8. The back-off is measured against `out` via `utf8_safe_len`, NOT against the current run: a run that FITS the budget can still end mid-character at a `fill_buf` boundary, leaving the half-written char in a previous chunk where a run-local check can't see it. (2) **`\uXXXX`** — the four hex digits are ordinary bytes inside a bulk-copied run, so `hex_left` walks them byte-wise and forbids a cut. (3) **Surrogate pairs** — `serde_json` rejects a lone `\uD83D`, so a cut after a high surrogate waits for its partner (`hex_value`). (4) **All-escape strings** — escape bytes are emitted without consulting the budget (that is what keeps them whole), so a string with no plain runs would never reach the bulk path where eliding is decided, and would stream in full. BOTH escape forms need the check and only the `\uXXXX` one had it at first: two-char escapes (`\n`, `\t`, `\"`, `\\`) had none, and a 40 KB body of `\n` sailed past a 1 KB cap — found in review, because the test covering this hazard used `\u200d` and so only ever exercised the hex path. Blank-line-heavy dumps and commands printing JSON are exactly the runaway shapes at issue. Hazards 2-4 are reachable on real data (the measured rollout carries `\uD83D` pairs, `‍` joiners, and `` from terminal output). The closing quote is always emitted so an elided string still terminates. Truncating an over-long object KEY is harmless and deliberately not guarded against: every key anything here looks up is short.
  - **Write the escape tests with doubled backslashes in ORDINARY strings** (`"\\uD83D\\uDE00"`), so the body really holds the six ASCII bytes. The first version of that test used raw strings containing actual emoji / control characters, never reached the escape path at all, and **passed with the guard deleted**. Same lesson as the `auto_sync_*` tests: mutate before believing. `read_record` splits on `\n` and does NOT strip `\r` the way `str::lines()` did — equivalent only because a stray `\r` is JSON whitespace, which `read_record_handles_crlf_like_the_lines_iterator_it_replaced` pins for Windows.
  - **The DETAIL path caps at `DETAIL_MAX_STRING_BYTES` = 1 MiB, and 1 MiB is CODEX'S OWN NUMBER (LOCKED).** It is `DEFAULT_OUTPUT_BYTES_CAP` (`codex-rs/utils/pty/src/lib.rs:12`), which `EXEC_OUTPUT_MAX_BYTES` (`core/src/exec.rs:76`) applies to everything Codex retains from a command — its comment gives the same reason we have: *"so a single runaway command cannot OOM the process by dumping huge amounts of data to stdout/stderr"*. Current Codex also renders only `TOOL_CALL_MAX_LINES` = **5** lines of tool output in its TUI (`tui/src/exec_cell/render.rs:33`; 50 for a user-invoked shell), and its live buffer caps at 1 MiB / 50 head + 50 tail lines / ~10 KB per line (`exec_cell/live_output.rs:5-11`). So this is **alignment with the official tool, not the divergence it first looks like** — Termory at 1 MiB still shows vastly more than the tool that wrote the file. Verified against codex `5729546` (2026-08-07); the `.audit-sources/codex` clone was 6 weeks stale when this was checked, so re-fetch before trusting it.
  - **It only ever bites HISTORICAL data.** Codex now truncates at write time, so a record it writes today cannot reach the cap. Measured on the machine this was found on — max line per month: 2026/03 **156 MB**, 04 5.3 MB, 05 55 KB, 06 42 KB, 07 41 KB; only 4 sessions hold a line over 1 MiB and ONE of them accounts for 221 MB of the 231 MB total.
  - **What the cap actually buys is SEARCH, not "that session opens" (LOCKED — do not "simplify" it away as a fix for one old session).** `search_sessions` calls `get_session` for every record, so the 141 MB message was materialized on **every query regardless of what was searched for**: measured 31 MB → **1103 MB** resident and 3.2 s for one search, against **293 MB / 2.4 s** after. Opening the session itself went ~900 MB → 313 MB, its IPC payload 234 MB → 17 MB, its largest message 141 MB → 1.9 MB.
  - **Unlike Codex, which truncates silently (`stdout_text.truncate`), Termory says so.** A record that lost bytes emits an `elided_notice` — `*[147.9 MB elided — open the original file to view the full output]*` — as its OWN message, pushed after that record's messages so it lands under the card whose output was cut. Two reasons it is a separate message rather than an append: `merge_tool_outputs` folds tool output INTO a code fence, where an italic notice renders as literal asterisks; and its `kind::ELIDED` keeps it out of `kind::TEXT`, which is what the LIST scan counts — a notice that counted would make the card disagree with its own transcript on exactly the sessions this touches. The figure is RAW JSON bytes dropped from the record (escapes included), not decoded characters.
  - **Every newline ends a record, `in_string` or not.** A raw newline cannot appear inside a valid JSON string, so for well-formed input this is identical to gating on `!in_string` — and for MALFORMED input it is the difference between losing one record and losing the whole rest of the file, since an unterminated string otherwise swallows every following newline as content. That resynchronisation is what `BufRead::lines()` gave for free; gating it away was a silent-data-loss regression caught in review.
  - **The notice fires only when the record actually rendered something.** Codex persists a turn as BOTH a `response_item` and an `event_msg`, and the `response_item` copy deliberately renders nothing (its twin is canonical) — so an ungated notice reported the same 1.4 MB loss TWICE, the first time with no card above it. `session_meta` and Claude's NULL_RENDERING attachments orphaned it the same way. A record that surfaced nothing hid nothing.
  - **The cap has DOWNSTREAM consequences, and they are the reason to think twice before raising it (or before treating this as a pure perf change).** `search_sessions` matches against `detail.messages[].text`, which is the CAPPED text — so **content past the cap is no longer findable**. Favourites store what the message list rendered (`toggleFavorite(selected, message, index)`), so starring an over-cap message snapshots the truncated body. Neither was noticed when the cap went in; both were found only when someone asked what else touched these paths. What is NOT affected, checked rather than assumed: `first_match_index` (search and the detail view both go through `get_session`, so the indices agree), `message_count` on the list card (`kind::ELIDED` is not `kind::TEXT`), `merge_tool_outputs` (it matches tool kinds only), and the frontend, which declares `SessionMessage.kind` in `types.ts` but reads it nowhere.
  - `read_record` returns `Option<u64>` — `None` at end of input, `Some(dropped_bytes)` for a record. Deliberately not a `bool` plus an out-param, which was the first shape: that forced the two scan sites to declare a variable purely to discard (they keep no text), and left the detail sites reading a *mutable outer* `elided` that could in principle carry a stale count into the next record's notice. `while let Some(elided)` scopes it per iteration so it cannot. Its accounting is **signed**: the UTF-8 back-off can reach below the point where this run started writing, when the half-written character came from an earlier chunk, so an unsigned subtraction underflows — caught by `read_record_elides_safely_when_a_character_straddles_a_chunk_edge`, which is also the test for that hazard.
- **`SearchCache` is bounded on BYTES, not just entry count (LOCKED).** Entries are whole parsed transcripts whose sizes differ by four orders of magnitude, so a 200-*entry* limit bounded nothing: with 158 sessions one search cached every one of them permanently, **+421 MB** resident, 224 MB of it a single session. `SEARCH_CACHE_BYTE_BUDGET` (256 MB) caps the total and `SEARCH_CACHE_MAX_ENTRY_BYTES` (32 MB) refuses an outsized transcript outright — it is served to the caller (which already holds the `Arc`) but never retained, since caching it would evict the entire rest of the cache to hold one item. The budget is deliberately generous rather than tight: search re-parses on a miss, so a budget that thrashes just trades this memory for a full re-parse per keystroke.
- **A macOS `malloc_zone_pressure_relief` reclaim was tried and REMOVED — it does nothing here.** Called at the end of `scan_sessions` it moved RSS not at all (903 → 903 MB after a detail load, 1485 → 1485 after a search): `malloc_default_zone()` returns the nano zone, not the scalable zone the large allocations live in. Making it work needs `malloc_get_all_zones` and more `unsafe` with no evidence it would help either. If the retained-spike question comes back (it only matters for the deferred detail/search paths above), swapping the global allocator for one that purges by design is the better lever than more zone plumbing.
- **Records page perf** (`App.tsx` detail effect + `src/components/MessageList.tsx`):
  - Detail load `useEffect` depends on `selected.source / path / id / updated_at / message_count` — narrow per-field deps instead of the whole `selected` object, so watcher-driven `applyScanResult` rebuilds that hand back a new `AppSession` reference with the same identity don't re-trigger `load_session`. A `prevSelectedKeyRef` distinguishes "new selection" (show `Loader2`) from "same selection, content advanced" (silent swap via `React.startTransition(setDetail)`).
  - `applyScanResult` no longer auto-selects `result[0]` on first launch — Records opens with an empty detail pane so app boot doesn't pay the cost of parsing the most recent session up front.
  - `setRoute` is wrapped in `React.useTransition` so clicking a rail icon (especially leaving a long Records detail) doesn't get blocked by the heavy unmount + re-render of the next route.
  - The detail pane renders messages through `<MessageList>` (`@tanstack/react-virtual`) which keeps roughly the visible window + 6-row overscan in the DOM regardless of session length. `measureElement` reports actual rendered heights so scrolling and the scrollbar stay accurate across variable-length message cards.
  - The `!selected && sessions.length === 0 && loading` "Scanning…" empty state was removed — initial boot just shows "Nothing to view yet" until data arrives, no transient spinner flash.
- `AppSession.preview` carries comma-separated tool tags (e.g. `"codex,opencode"` for AGENTS.md). The list-card `MemoryCard` renders one brand badge per tag via `memoryToolsOf()`; the detail-header badge renders a single type label (`Session` / `Memory` / `Skill`) via `typeLabelOf()`.
- For session-type entries the detail header shows the GUID (`selected.id`) on its own line below the project path, rendered as inline monospace via Tailwind (`font-mono` / `text-xs` / `text-muted-foreground`). Memory/Skill entries omit the GUID line.
- Project-level `AGENTS.md` and `AGENTS.override.md` are always tagged with both `codex` and `opencode` regardless of which tool actually has sessions in the cwd. Rationale: the AGENTS.md spec is tool-neutral — Termory reports which tools CAN read the file, not which tool happened to run there. Verified by `scan_memory_always_tags_project_agents_md_with_both_codex_and_opencode` test.
- Sidebar source filter (Codex/Claude/Gemini/OpenCode/All) applies to **all three** panes (Sessions, Memory, Skills).
